@@118825880 <h> Flood Risks in Kenya : How Landscape Shapes the Disasters <p> Kenya 's natural landscape , which encompasses rivers , lakes , hills and escarpments , heightens its vulnerability to flooding because of topography . Beyond these natural factors , however , human-made causes exacerbate this risk , exposing everyone in nearby areas . So , what are these aspects , and how do they contribute to flooding ? <p> Flooding remains one of Kenya 's significant climatic hazards , with its triggers rooted in natural and man-made factors . During every rainfall season , the country grapples with the perennial issue of displaced people , damaged infrastructure or overflowing rivers and lakes . <p> So , what are the reasons for the vulnerability of floods ? Understanding the relation between landscape topography , human activities , and climate behaviour explains this vulnerability . <h> Natural characteristics that make Kenya prone to floods <p> Some areas affected by floods in Kenya , including the Kano Plains , Budalangi , and the Tana River , share similar features that make them susceptible to flooding . <h> Low-lying areas <p> The Kano Plains lie between 1160 and 1300 metres above sea level , Budalangi Plain is 1,130 meters above sea level , and the Tana River is between zero and 37 metres above sea level . As a result , this lack of elevation leads to inefficiency in draining water , causing these areas to submerge whenever it rains . <p> Similarly , on the other side of the country , the coastal areas of Mombasa , Kilifi , and Lamu suffered a fate similar to that of other low-lying terrains . For instance , Mombasa lies 50 metres above sea level , Kilifi ranges between 0 and 100 metres above sea level , and Lamu is 0 metres above sea level . <p> As a result , during the 2024 El Nino enhanced rainfall , these regions experienced some of the worst flooding in the country . <h> Heavy rainfall in upper catchment areas <p> Mount Kenya holds 11 shrinking glaciers said to be rapidly melting due to the burning of fossil fuels , fires , and deforestation . Photo : Ed RamSource : Getty Images <p> The low-lying areas are surrounded by steep slopes and highlands that receive heavy rainfall , which drains into them . <p> For example , the waters of the Nzoia River that end up in Budalangi Plains come from its upper catchment areas of Cherangany Hills ( 3,350 metres above sea level ) and Mount Elgon ( 4.321 metres ) . <p> Additionally , Cherangany has an unimodal rainfall pattern and receives substantial rains ranging between 1,200 and 1,500 mm annually , while Mt . Elgon has an annual rainfall of around 1,400 and 1,800 mm . <p> Similarly , the Kano Plains is surrounded by the Nandi Hills , which rise to 1,478 metres above sea level , and the Nyabondo escarpments . Most of the plains ' rainfall comes from the northern side of the Nandi Hills , which receives approximately 1200 to 2000mm annual rainfall . <h> Plains and towns integration with river systems <p> River systems that meander through the plains and towns also cause flooding across Kenya . For example , the Nzoia River passes through the Budalangi Plains on its way to Lake Victoria , and the Nyando and Nyamasaria Rivers flow through the Kano Plains . <p> Similarly , the Tana River passes through Garissa , Garsen , and Hola towns before reaching the Indian Ocean . Consequently , these plains and towns remain susceptible to flooding when the rivers overflow . <h> Rising lakes and ocean water levels <p> The Nyando and Nzoia rivers flow through the Kano and Budalangi plains and drain into Lake Victoria . In 2024 , the water levels in Africa 's largest freshwater lake reached 13.66 metres , up from 13.5 metres in 2023 , leading to the surrounding areas getting submerged . Furthermore , this backflow causes the rivers ' already full banks to overflow , flooding the plains . <p> Similarly , the Tana River empties into the Indian Ocean through the Tana River Delta , and any overflow has led to the waters spilling back into Tana and the surrounding areas . In addition , rising ocean water levels worsen the situation for residents of Mombasa , Kilifi , and the surrounding areas . <p> According to the World Meteorological Organisation ( WMO ) , sea levels have risen twofold since 1993 , with an increase of 10 millimetres since January 2020 . This is not a positive indication unless measures are taken to mitigate the rising water levels and stop it from pushing the shores further inland . <h> Presence of black cotton soil <p> The black cotton soil along the Nyando , Nzoia , and Tana rivers worsens the flooding problem . This soil absorbs water and expands but drains its waters slowly . Furthermore , its presence along the river slows water movement , leading to waterlogging and further flooding . <h> Human activities that increase flood vulnerability <p> An excavator is marooned in flood water near homes that their owners have abandoned at Nairobi 's plush Runda estate following recent heavy rainfall in Nairobi . Photo : Tony Karumba.Source : Getty Images <p> Kenyans have also contributed to the flooding problem by undermining the environment through deforestation , poor land management , and other practices . As a result , the country repeatedly faces similar challenges before , during , and after every rainfall season . <h> Deforestation of major water catchment areas <p> The Mau Complex , the largest water catchment in Kenya , continues to face significant deforestation . Forest Monitoring Groups report that 25% of its forest cover was lost due to human pressures between 1984 and 2020 . <p> As a result , this degradation affects the rivers flowing from the Mau , including the Nzoia , Tana , Ewaso Nyiro , and Molo , by weakening soil retention and leading to high surface runoff that carries sediments . <p> Consequently , this obstructs the natural flow of water , causing waterlogging and flooding . Additionally , deforestation causes the water from the highlands to drain quickly into the lower-lying areas , triggering flash floods and landslides . <p> Similarly , the Aberdare Ranges faces a comparable threat , with approximately 10 million hectares lost yearly and 70 million hectares lost to fires . Like the Mau Complex , it is an important water catchment area for the Tana River , whose waters are used for hydroelectric generation . The region faces similar impacts like poor soil retention , quick surface runoff and sedimentation . <h> Urbanisation <p> The Kenyan Population and Housing Census 2019 estimated that Nairobi 's population is 4.5 million , a far larger number than the initially expected 500,000 residents . <p> As a result , rapid population growth has created pressure to expand infrastructure , encroaching on riparian lands and creating impervious surfaces such as roads . <p> In an exclusive interview with Tuko.co.ke , Oscar Omoke , Chief Officer of Water and Sanitation , Nairobi County , said that Nairobi 's steep terrain makes it susceptible to flooding during heavy downpours . <p> Nairobi has a steeply sloping terrain making it susceptible to flooding . This makes it difficult to manage the situation when it rains heavily because the terrain tends to naturally activate and accelerate the velocity of the floods.In April , we had heavy downpours in the city , and we experienced a lot of challenges because the rains came with massive damage . Several pipes supplying water to Nairobi residents were broken . One line running from Gigiri reservoir through Karura , Outering road all the way to Eastern Bypass was destroyed.This pipe was connected to another one supplying water to the CBD and another one to people living along Mombasa Road . There was no water in more than 10 densely populated estates , yet we had heavy rains <p> For example , Kibera , the largest informal settlement in Africa , has over 250,000 residents . Most of these houses lie along the Nairobi River floodplain , and during rainy seasons , they are washed away , forcing residents to evacuate . <p> Furthermore , these areas suffer from poor or nonexistent drainage systems , which are easily overwhelmed during heavy rains . In addition , poor waste disposal practices have led to dumping in drainage systems and open sewers clogging the system , exacerbating flooding . <p> Beyond informal settlements , irregular construction around the city has led to the encroachment of riparian lands , disrupting the water flow and causing perennial flooding . The Institution of Surveyors in Kenya estimates that over 5,000 structures are built on riparian lands , damaging ecosystems . <h> Over-cultivation <p> Sand mining is one of the human activities that has damaged Nzoia 's riverbed . The growing demand for sand has led people to harvest it excessively , causing lasting damage to the ecosystem . As a result , the land remains bare , with its water table depleted , thus losing its biodiversity and widening the riverbed . <p> In addition , practices such as quarrying have left Nzoia with bottomless pits , further depleting the water table . Consequently , the vegetation that depends on water is dying , eliminating wetlands and reducing the land 's ability to absorb moisture . <h> Soil erosion <p> Soil erosion removes the top layer of the soil , depleting the nutrients and slowly causing the vegetation to wither . This process damages the soil 's stability by lowering its water-absorbing abilities , leading to quick water flow and flash floods . <p> Moreover , the eroded soil is carried into rivers , causing sedimentation that obstructs the natural flow of water and slows it down . As a result , the soil 's capacity to retain water is further reduced , increasing the chances of flooding . <h> How climate patterns exacerbate flood risks <p> An aerial view shows the damage caused by a main road and houses submerged in water following heavy floods in Garissa . Photo : Luis TatoSource : Getty Images <p> Climate patterns dictate the type of seasons the country experiences and the intensity of flooding that may occur . For this reason , understanding the relationship between weather and flooding is essential to comprehending its environmental impact . <h> Heavy rainfall <p> Kenya experiences a bimodal climate pattern , meaning it has two distinct rainfall seasons . The first is the long rains , which happen between March and May , and the second is the short rains , which occur between October and December . In both seasons , rainfall intensity varies and can sometimes be heavier or lesser than expected . <p> For example , El Nino in Kenya has led to above-average rainfall , which has resulted in floods . In a climate outlook for the different rainfall seasons in 2024 , the Kenya Meteorological Department ( KMD ) noted that several parts of the country , including the Lake Victoria Basin , the Highlands West of the Rift Valley , and Central , would have above-average rainfall . This demonstrates the impact of the weather phenomenon on rain intensity . <p> Similarly , during the short rain period of October , November , and December 2023 , the country experienced enhanced rainfall influenced by the Indian Ocean Dipole . <p> This phenomenon , which is the difference in sea surface temperature between the West and East of the Indian Ocean , pushes moisture inland when warmer , resulting in heavy rainfall . <p> Consequently , people living in the lowlands were urged to move to higher ground because of the risk that rivers , flood plains , and lakes could overflow . Moreover , the quick movement of the water , carrying loose soil from higher ground , increased the likelihood of landslides and flash floods . <h> Extreme weather patterns like El Nino events <p> The El Nino weather phenomenon is caused by water warming along the equator around the Eastern Pacific . This leads to the build-up of warm moisture in the atmosphere , which develops into rain . <p> For example , one of the most famous floods due to the 1997/98 and 2023/24 El Nino events resulted in untold damage to infrastructure , human lives , and livestock . <h> Drought cycles <p> A destructive rainfall season leads to drought , as people 's harvest gets washed away in the resulting flood . Conversely , during this extreme heat , the soil hardens and reduces its ability to absorb water . Consequently , when it rains again , the soil infiltration rate is low , hence flooding . <p> For instance , parts of Northern Kenya , such as Garissa and Wajir , experienced a prolonged drought before the 2023/24 El Nino-induced rains . When the rains finally arrived , floods swept across the region , displacing multiple people . <h> The Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone <p> The Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone ( ITCZ ) is a low-pressure region around the equator where the Northern and Southern Hemisphere trade winds converge . This convergence causes warm , moist air to rise , cooling and condensing to form rainfall . <p> In Kenya , the ITCZ significantly affects rainfall patterns . When it carries most air Southwards , it causes significant rainfall , particularly around Lake Victoria and coastal regions . <p> Conversely , during the long rain season , its northward movement results in heavy downpours , particularly in the central , eastern , and Western parts of Kenya . <p> Unfortunately , the intensity of these rains quickly overwhelms areas unprepared for high amounts of rainfall , increasing flood vulnerability . <h> Indian Ocean Dipole <p> The Indian Ocean Dipole is the difference between the sea surface temperatures of the Western and Eastern regions of the Indian Ocean . <p> In 2023 , the Kenya Meteorological Department announced a positive signal at the equator , meaning the Western region was warmer than the Eastern region . This led to enhanced rainfall during the October , November , and December seasons , which caused widespread flooding . <p> Similarly , October to December 2019 ranks amongst East Africa 's wettest period in 40 years . The above-average rainfall was attributed to the long-lasting effects of the positive Indian Ocean Dipole , one of the strongest on record . <h> Sustainable land use practices to reduce flood risk in Kenya <p> Given the recurring issue of floods , proper land management practices must be enforced to mitigate the risk . While the government and the people are aware of the environmental damage , strict policy implementation is necessary to reduce deaths and destruction . These are some of the practices that should be enforced . <h> Afforestation and reforestation <p> Kenya 's forest cover is 8% compared to the global average of 30% . This loss has led to severe consequences during the rainy season , including increased flooding . To mitigate these impacts , the country works with partners to restore the forest covers around major water towers . <p> Several projects are ongoing , including the Eden People+Planet , which is focused on the Kijabe Forest in the Rift Valley and the Northern Coast , and the 20 Million Tree Campaign , which targets damaged areas in five of Kenya 's water towers : Mt . Kenya , Mt . Elgon , Mau Complex , Aberdares , and Cherangani Hills . <p> In addition , the Kenyan government launched an ambitious campaign to plant about 15 billion trees in 10 years to achieve the targeted forest cover . This aligns with its policies , the Kenya Forest Policy 2014 , Land Act 2012 , Forest Conservation and Management Act ( 2016 ) and the National Climate Change Response Strategy , all of which emphasise forest protection and tree planting . <p> In 2023 , the country also marked its first Tree Planting Day national holiday to promote tree planting and environmental conservation . Furthermore , the government committed over $80 million in the current financial year to promote the exercise . <h> Restoration of wetlands <p> In 2023 , the Kenyan government launched the Wetlands Restoration Program , part of its initiative to build 15 billion trees to combat the devastating effects of climate change . <p> Through the Ministry of Environment , Climate Change , and Forestry , in collaboration with the Kenya Forest Service and the National Environment and Management Authority , the government has identified 10,000 wetlands countrywide , including lakes , swamps , rivers , marshes , and riparian zones . It plans to revive them in the next 10 years . <p> Over the years , the government has demonstrated its will in restoring the country 's wetlands . For example , in 2018 , Southend Mall along Lang'ata Road was demolished because it was built on the Ngong-river bed . Similarly , other buildings , including Nakumatt UKay and Shell Petrol Station in Westlands , were brought down . <p> According to the Nairobi Regeneration Team and the National Environmental Authority ( NEMA ) , about 4,000 buildings were on riparian lands in Nairobi . However , they did not disclose the buildings . <h> Proper urban planning <p> The population explosion across the country has caused a surge in demand for urban expansion . Consequently , irregular structures have mushroomed , ranging from improper buildings to informal settlements . All of these have interfered with the drainage systems by blocking or overwhelming them . <p> To remedy this , city planning must incorporate green infrastructure that can help absorb water , such as parks , gardens , and open spaces . Furthermore , new construction must be regulated to avoid structures that block the drainage system or lack proper drainage systems . <p> In addition , current drainage systems must be maintained and upgraded to meet current demands . Regular cleaning and proper waste disposal , which often results in drainage , will help reduce flooding . <h> Construction of water management systems <p> Flood-prone areas need dams and other water reservoirs to manage excess water . In this regard , Kenya has several water dams across the country that contain excess rainwater , with the Seven Forks dam being one of the most prominent . <p> However , the surrounding areas within the Tana River still experience flooding despite the dams ' existence . To address this , the government aims to add more dams , including Mutonga and Low Grand Falls . <p> In contrast , despite perennial flooding , the government 's desire to build a dam in Budalangi has yet to materialise . It proposed Kimilili as the ideal site for a dam , but the residents and local leaders opposed the move amid fears of losing their homes , even though the government assured them of compensation . <h> Soil conservation <p> Flooding damages the soil 's ability to retain moisture . However , this can be reversed using several techniques , including mulching , ridges and tie-ridges <p> One such technique is ridging , which involves building earth banks on the contour of a slope . During the rainy season , water accumulates on the ridge and slowly infiltrates the soil . Clay soil is preferred when building , as it offers stability . <p> Moreover , the ridge should be between 20 and 30cm . Ridges are effective as they reduce runoff and soil erosion . However , they should be carefully erected ; otherwise , they might exacerbate soil erosion . <p> Another technique is mulching , which involves placing material sheets on the surface of cultivated soil . This helps preserve the soil 's moisture and improve its texture and structure . Although plastic is preferred because it is non-biodegradable , it is not used in Kenya because of the country 's ban on plastics . <p> Overall , this method is also helpful in retaining soil moisture , hence its ability to absorb water even in flooding conditions and keep the land productive . <h> Final word <p> Kenya 's landscape has contributed to the country 's flooding disasters , but human-induced activities have also worsened the problem . When people encroach on wetlands to build or conduct other activities , they interfere with water catchment areas . As a result , the water 's path is diverted to different places , leading to flooding . Consequently , these poor land management practices have resulted in flooding . However , robust legislation and implementation can reverse the damage and restore the country 's ecosystem . <p> Tuko.co.ke published an article detailing roads frequently affected by floods . During the rainy season , these routes were impassable as water reached unprecedented levels . This information is essential to have alternatives whenever needed . Check out the article for more . 
@@118825881 <p> The Kenya Meteorological Department ( KMD ) is crucial in weather preparedness , particularly during adverse weather conditions . It allows people to be aware of the dangers the weather poses and prepare adequately . As flooding continues to wreak havoc countrywide , understanding KMD 's role in flood management is essential . <p> The KMD operates under the Ministry of Environment , Climate Change , and Forestry . Among its functions is helping prevent adverse weather impacts and severe climate events . To do this , it employs different systems and collaborates with different bodies to convey the latest information to the public . So , what tools do they use , and are these strategies working ? <p> In an exclusive interview with Tuko.co.ke , Bernard Chanzu , Deputy Director of Forecasting Services at Kenya Meteorological Department , reiterated that on top of using KMD 's framework , they collaborate with other entities to ensure everything is streamlined . <p> According to him , the government is at the forefront of ensuring that it utilizes all mechanisms and systems available to mitigate the negative impacts of heavy rainfall . <p> We share early warnings to prepare the public for any eventuality . Our team uses satellite data , radars , and ground-based weather stations to provide timely and accurate weather forecasts . The government and other stakeholders , like the Kenya Red Cross , use this information to devise strategies to mitigate the impact of heavy rains . Our data is scientific and specific in that we can predict when and where it will rain and of what intensity . <p> In 2023 , the KMD issued more than five severe weather forecasts to the government and public to help prepare and mitigate disaster . What systems did they use to arrive at these conclusions ? <h> Heavy rainfall advisories <p> The department relies on heavy rainfall advisories ( HRA ) to warn the public about possible flooding when intense rainfall is expected . These are text-based alerts issued by the weatherman when rainfall is expected to exceed 30mm within a 24-hour window . <p> The information shared through HRAs is drawn from the dynamic atmospheric models from the World Meteorological Organization ( WMO ) Global Producing Centres ( GPCs ) and the Weather Research and Forecasting models operated at KMD 's offices . <p> Once the advisories are ready , they are issued within 72 hours and only include details about the affected counties and general information for flood preparedness . <p> According to Bernard Chanzu , the Kenya Meteorological Department partners with institutions like the National Disaster Operations Center , the Kenya Red Cross , the Ministry of Environment and County Governments to map flood-prone areas . <p> We collaborate with other partners like the National Disaster Operations Center , the Kenya Red Cross , the Ministry of Environment and county governments to map flood-prone areas . This helps response teams to prioritise areas that need urgent interventions . That is why plans such as clearing waterways , constructing dams , building dikes , moving vulnerable families to safe places and so forth , start in advance . This is a very critical role the met department plays and ca n't be overlooked . <p> The Met Department also conducts regular workshops and campaigns to sensitise the public on the dangers of floods and how to mitigate their impact . <p> Once released , the HRAs are shared among government agencies , such as the Ministry of Interior and Coordination of National Government and humanitarian organisations like the Kenya Red Cross Society ( KRCS ) . <h> National monitoring and risk assessment <p> The Water Resources Authority ( WRA ) works with KMD to monitor water levels to prevent flooding and help with flood management . It has identified 21 flood-prone areas , including river basins and the urban regions . <p> To do this , it has 223 surface water monitoring stations , which collect information on water levels , flow , and quality in rivers , lakes , and reservoirs and monitor water availability , flooding , and drought potential . <p> In addition to the surface water monitoring stations , WRA has 260 climatic stations that collect data on windspeed , rainfall , temperature and humidity using its 40 telemetric stations . <p> Water levels in rivers and lakes are monitored twice daily , and climate data is monitored once daily . Over the years , WRA has upgraded its technology , from automatic chart recorders to logging stations , and is working to automate its process to get real-time data . <p> In addition , it installed the Water Resources Information Management System ( WRIMS ) , which allows it to do flood and drought forecasting and data management . <h> Ground and satellite-based observational networks <p> With the help of international organisations like the Famine Early Warning Systems Network ( FEWS Net ) , KMD gathers data and provides daily weather forecasts . Satellites use sensors to capture data from the Earth , which is then transmitted back to the Earth for analysis . <p> This process relies on two types of satellites : geostationary satellites , which monitor one part of the equator and give data on the same region , making cloud formation and real-time weather tracking possible , and polar-orbiting satellites , which produce global weather data . These systems generate accurate forecasts and monitor weather events like drought and floods . <h> Standardised Precipitation Index ( PSI ) <p> The Standardised Precipitation Index ( PSI ) is another early warning system KMD uses to forecast weather . It monitors and measures precipitation during a particular period , allowing weather forecasters to predict the likelihood of floods based on precipitation levels . <p> Specifically , SPI is used to analyse deviations in rainfall patterns , and a high positive SPI , usually above 1.0 , indicates the potential for severe rainfall , hence the possibility of flooding . <h> Accuracy of recent flood predictions and their measurements <p> Pedestrians try to pass a flooded street following heavy rains at Kiembeni district in Mombasa on November 17 , 2023 . Photo : Stringer.Source : Getty Images <p> Making accurate flood predictions is a complex process requiring real-time data . In 2023 , KMD accurately predicted El Ni ? o in Kenya and its subsequent impact , including flooding . In an El Ni ? o update dated 30 July 2023 , it said in part : <p> " Based on model predictions and expert assessment , there is a very high likelihood ( approximately 90% chance ) of El Ni ? o prevailing during the remaining part of the year and may extend into early 2024 . " <p> Similarly , in 2021 , it correctly predicted floods in Marsabit , Mandera , and Wajir counties and issued an advisory . As a result , widespread flooding was experienced across the country during the long rainy seasons of May , June , and July . <h> How flooding forecast accuracy is measured <p> KMD employs various methods to assess the accuracy of their predictions , including : <h> Verifying against observed data <p> KMD corroborates its meteorological and hydrological forecasts using satellite data , which it uses to produce statistical data . These data sets are used to identify inaccuracies and provide the most accurate prediction . <h> Using statistical metrics <p> As noted , satellite observations produce statistics used to gauge forecast accuracy . These include the hit rate , which indicates a correct prediction , and the false alarm rate , which indicates that a weather event has not been predicted . <p> In addition , the critical success index , also known as the threat score , is a combination of the hit rate and the false alarm rate used to determine flood forecasting . <h> Tropical Africa model <p> KMD uses the Tropical Africa model , provided by the United Kingdom MET office , to enable more accurate predictions . This model is the highest resolution available for weather forecasting in tropical Africa , meaning it can provide data on localised weather events such as heavy rainfall . <h> Public perception of KMD 's effectiveness in flood management efforts <p> The Met Department issues weather alerts regularly and disseminates these notices through various mediums to stakeholders , who should take action to ensure flood management . <p> Despite these efforts , a Geopoll survey conducted between April 30th and May 1st , 2024 , targeting 1,755 respondents , revealed that most respondents attributed flood management responsibilities to the government rather than specifically to KMD . <p> According to the survey , 32% of the respondents believe that the national government is entirely responsible for flood management and prevention , 29% said the county governments should bear the responsibility , and 17% stated it is a personal responsibility . <p> Furthermore , 39% of the respondents opined that the government 's flood management efforts were ineffective or very ineffective , almost 20% said they were effective , and less than 15% graded them as very effective . <p> In terms of flood awareness , 44% of the responders said they were well-informed before , during , and after flooding events . Meanwhile , 21% reported not being aware of any measures or having little knowledge about them , and 34% disclosed they were somewhat informed . <p> Additionally , another report by the International Center for Humanitarian Affairs indicated that 58% of communities recognise the Kenya Red Cross as the primary source of early warning information , compared to KMD and county government , which scored 4% . Local leaders also recognised KRC more prominently , although 63% of the respondents acknowledged KMD 's role . <h> Other factors impacting public perception of KMD 's role in flood management <p> Beyond the widespread lack of authority among the public , the following factors lower KMD 's credibility . <h> Lack of streamlined information <p> In October 2023 , Kenyans needed clarification about the accuracy of the El Nino warnings after President William Ruto downscaled the severity of KMD 's prediction and said it would not happen . This statement led to confusion and laxity , slowing down citizens ' and government 's preparation for the potentially threatening event . <p> In response , KMD was forced to clarify the matter . Its Director-General , David Gikungu , apologised for the confusion but stood by their initial assessments . <p> " As the director , I did my part in preparedness for El Ni ? o . We have issued forecasts and given information in English and Swahili . We have attended meetings on various levels , and we even have county directors of meteorology who downscale the forecast for their people by providing information to users even in their local language , " he said . <h> Personal experiences leading to disregarding flood warnings <p> KMD collaborates with media outlets to decimate weather forecast warnings through text , print , radio , and digital outlets . Despite these efforts , Dr Gikungu noted during a November 2023 interview with Spice FM Kenya that some people sometimes disregard the warnings and wait for the situation to worsen before taking action . He explained that this behaviour stems from previous experiences where severe flooding was predicted , but only mild floods occurred . <h> Channels of receiving weather information <p> KMD disseminates weather alerts using various channels , including television , radio , short message service , print , and social media . To ensure a wider reach , it liaises with partners . <p> According to a GeoPoll survey , 43% of the respondents got their weather updates through social media and 40% through television . Meanwhile , radio was the third most popular medium to get weather updates , with 14% , and only 2% through community leaders . <h> Recent success stories or failures that highlight the department 's role <p> Over the years , KMD has accumulated notable successes and failures that underscore its role in flood management , information gathering , and sharing . <h> KMD 's success <p> The department has made notable milestones in the course of its operations , including : <h> Recognition by the World Meteorological Organisation ( WMO ) <p> In 2023 , the World Meteorological Organisation ( WMO ) issued KMD certifications in recognition of its Voi and Lamu observing stations after surpassing 100 years of supplying accurate meteorological information . These stations were inducted into the WMO Centennial Observing Stations as KMD joined an illustrious list that includes countries such as the United States . <h> Partnerships that enhance forecasting <p> KMD has partnered with international organisations to strengthen its weather forecasting abilities . For example , it has been incorporating research and development from the Science for Weather Information and Forecasting Techniques ( SWIFT ) in its forecasting operations and implements its response to severe weather . <p> Some of the most tangible aspects of this partnership include organising testbeds for a 0-6 hours forecasting , 2-4 weeks defined as seasonal to sub-seasonal forecasting , and facilitating the use of EUMETSAT nowcasting , which KND uses in making its daily weather forecasts -- additionally , SWIFT mentors , engagement and training events . <h> Climate risk reporting <p> In addition to preparing daily and seasonal forecasts , KMD is studying climate change and its impact on weather conditions in the country . For example , it published a " State of the Climate 2023 " report aiming to influence policy and decision-making in several areas , including strengthening local and national early warning systems , building flood-resistant infrastructure , and diversifying the production of agricultural goods . <h> Approval of bills and policies by parliament <p> In 2023 , the Kenyan government approved the Meteorological Bill and Meteorological Policy , giving it a more descriptive title , the Kenya Meteorological Services ( KMS ) , capturing and acknowledging its importance in dealing with weather and its impacts . <p> Furthermore , it enables it to remain compliant with international instruments such as the 2015 African Ministerial Conference on Meteorology ( AMCOMET ) and address its issues and challenges . Additionally , the policy aims to develop a framework that will allow KMD to fulfil its mandate efficiently and in a structured way . <h> KMD failures <p> The department has sometimes fallen short of its mandate , raising concerns over its effectiveness . These are some of its failures . <h> Concerns over forecast accuracy <p> In 2023 , KMD came under scrutiny over its predictions of heavy rainfall and flooding in Nairobi and the Central region between November 30 - December 4 after they were inaccurate . Its Director , David Gikungu , gave a 90% probability of El Ni ? o from October to February to April . <p> As a result , other state agencies , like the Water Resources Authority ( WRA ) , advised people in flood-prone areas to evacuate . Still , it remains to be seen if they heeded these calls . <p> At the same time , President William Ruto refuted KMD 's claims of an impending El Ni ? o , causing further confusion . However , Gikungu insisted that their prediction was correct . <p> Similarly , between 2015 and 2019 , the Natural Hazards and Earth Systems Science documented that the heavy rainfall advisories issued in early 2015 , up to and including 2017 , needed to be more effective for flood preparedness or heavy rains . <p> The nine advisories did not lead to heavy rainfall as forecast . In contrast , heavy rainfall and floods occurred in counties that did not receive advisories . <p> Consequently , these failed predictions attract scrutiny to the reliability of KMD 's forecasts , creating doubt over KMD 's expertise and methods of operations and decreasing public trust . <h> Poor coordination with stakeholders <p> After the deadly 2024 floods , the government 's response to KMD 's early warning systems was faulted after hundreds lost their life before the president formed a response committee on April 24 , 2024 . <p> This followed Ruto 's downplaying of the severity of the warning , which delayed response . In response , the government promised the release of Ksh 10 billion to combat the crisis . However , it remains unclear how that money was used . <h> Little to no public recognition <p> As the International Center for Humanitarian Affairs ( ICHR ) highlighted , most communities recognise the Kenya Red Cross as the primary source of early warning information . <p> This indicates that many are unaware of KMD 's services and responsibility for issuing weather information . Additionally , many members of the public lack understanding of interpreting weather forecasts , hence ignoring them . <h> Lack of up-to-date technology <p> KMD relies on heavy rainfall advisories for flood forecasting , which provide general information regarding areas affected and lack details on flood impact . Additionally , they rely on manual equipment like the rain gauge to collect data . <p> While automatic logging stations and radar are available , their coverage is inadequate compared to the regions they need to cover , meaning real-time tracking of rainfall and storms is not possible . This prevents the ability to make localised and accurate short-term predictions . <h> Over-reliance on international organisations <p> KMD heavily relies on international partners , such as the FEWS Net , the World Meteorological Department ( WMO ) , and the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts , to create global forecasting models . <p> While this gives them important information , which they then scale down to a national level , it might not fully account for Kenya 's microclimate in lakes and mountainous regions . <h> Lack of accountability <p> Rarely does the public direct their frustrations over preparedness to KMD , even though they historically failed to issue warnings or were delayed . Many call out other government agencies , and KMD 's role has been forgotten . <p> In addition , the department seems to be hidden from the public , and this disengagement leads to misunderstandings over its mandate . <h> What improvements could the department make to enhance flood preparedness and community response ? <p> Residents of Mathare try to evacuate the area with their important belongings in Nairobi , Kenya . Photo : Gerald AndersonSource : Getty Images <p> KMD can make several improvements to lower flood risk and enhance its weather forecasting operations . They include : <h> Create and expand models for local hydrology <p> Although KMD 's heavy rainfall advisories have been efficient to a certain extent , they need to be able to monitor precipitation and evaporation to characterise flood impacts accurately . <p> In addition , KMD needs to establish a unified national flood modelling and forecasting system to provide a standardised view of flood risk in the country . Currently , only the Nzoia basin has a flood forecast system , but its partnership with the World Bank could change that . <p> Through its Water Security and Climate Resilience project program , it will support the implementation of river flood warning systems in other basins and upgrade the one at Nzoia basin . This will help provide more targeted flood forecasts . <h> Establish itself as an authoritative voice <p> ICHA reports showed that many people receive early warning alerts from the Red Cross or community leaders and hardly recognise KMD as the primary source of information . This disengagement affects understanding between the department and citizens , who may not acknowledge KMD 's alerts . <p> To combat this , the department should strive to create a direct outreach strategy , such as SMS , a weather forecast app , or a hotline , that facilitates direct engagement with the audience and establishes itself as the authoritative voice for weather updates . <p> Furthermore , KMD must conduct awareness campaigns to foster its relationship with the public and build trust . This will help improve weather forecast literacy and customise information for specific regions . <h> Ensure timely dissemination of alerts <p> Some affected individuals have reported not receiving weather alerts or receiving them late . To combat this , the department should release timely forecasts and coordinate with agencies to pass on information . <h> Strengthen collaboration with stakeholders <p> Limited coordination between KMD and risk management agencies has led to poor preparedness . The National Disaster Management Unit and the National Disaster Operation Centre are tasked with leading the efforts to prepare , respond , mitigate and recover nationally . <p> Meanwhile , county disaster management is tasked with these responsibilities at the county level . To seamlessly handle these duties , they need to create streamlined channels of operations to increase efficiency . <h> Develop a feedback mechanism <p> KMD needs to know what public responses to weather alerts are to assess the effectiveness of the messages they share . They can do this through surveys such as the one GeoPoll conducted . This will allow them to modify their strategies to incorporate the realities of the end user . <h> Improve transparency and accountability <p> The KMD website lacks publications and reports on the effectiveness of weather forecasts and the equipment they use to collect information . In this context , UNECA , in collaboration with the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development ( UNCTAD ) , has implemented a project funded by the Danish International Development Agency ( DANIDA ) to identify green value chain opportunities and climate adaptation needs across 19 African countries and five regions , including Kenya , " Dr. Abdallah disclosed .

On the one hand , exports have grown significantly , driven by growing international demand ; on the other hand , climate challenges have complicated production in key regions such as Piura , the country 's main producing area . <p> According to the CEO of Limones Piuranos , Peru exported 32,000 tons of Tahiti lime in 2023 , 30% more than in the previous year . This growth has been especially noticeable in Europe , where demand for fresh citrus fruits continues to increase . Tahiti lime exports have increased from 300 containers in 2018 to more than 1,300 in 2023 , with revenues exceeding $35 million . <p> Limones Piuranos has been a pioneer in the production and export of citrus fruits since 2003 , with more than 2,200 hectares dedicated to the production of Tahiti limes , Sutil limes , and mangoes . <p> " Tahiti limes are mostly destined for fresh consumption . 80% of the production is allocated to the international market and the rest to the domestic market . In contrast , the smaller , more acidic Sutil lime is mainly used to produce essential oils . This product is exported in 400-pound drums and is highly valued by the cosmetic and pharmaceutical industries . <p> Climate change has left its mark on Piura 's crops . The region , hit by phenomena such as El Ni ? o and the absence of rain , has experienced a water crisis that reduced the size of the limes , shrinking the proportion destined for the fresh market . " The high demand for refrigerated containers during the export season increases transport costs . However , the opening of the port of Chancay could improve logistics conditions in the future , optimizing costs and shipping times , " he stated . <p> Fresh Tahiti lime prices range between 1.30 and 1.50 dollars per kilo , while derived products , such as lemon oil , maintain stable prices after fluctuations caused by global shortages in previous years . <p> " Our processes are 100% natural and we reuse the peel waste for composting . This approach has been key to maintaining a competitive advantage over other major exporters such as Mexico and Brazil , " the CEO of Limones Piuranos stressed . <p> Peru 's Tahiti lime harvest is divided into two main seasons : January to April for the North American market and August to November for Europe . Despite how complicated this season looks , Peruvian exporters have high expectations for it . <p> " The company projects a 30% annual growth in its exports . With an additional 200 hectares of Tahiti lime in production , the company seeks to consolidate its presence in Europe and expand into new markets . Peruvian limes have enormous potential thanks to their quality and the fact that the country is free of HLB , a pest that affects other producers , " the CEO stressed .

unaffordable to make and sell to consumers . <p> The regulations seek to introduce multiple fees , such as Sh150 per item on all imported packaging materials and finished goods , five per cent fees with the Sh150 per item fees on imported packaging material in addition to the fees paid to PROs for post-consumer waste collection and management , that would amount to double taxation . <p> " This is also ambiguous as it does not expressly state what constitutes of an item , he added . <p> " At a fee of Sh150 per item , and with the broad categories of goods used in manufacturing that are targeted , the cost of production in the alcoholic beverages industry is going to increase by 70 per cent . " <p> Abak secretary Eric Kiniti said manufacturers and distributors of alcohol are also concerned about how the new regulations were developed as there was inadequate public participation . <p> " The lack of public participation in the formulation of regulations whose implementation could lead to a 70 per cent increase in the costs of production for the alcoholic beverages sector is a fatal sin of omission , " he said . <p> The draft that was published and is now before the Committee on Delegated Legislation included provisions that had not been discussed , Kiniti said . <p> " These include fees charged per item , which is ambiguous and open to abuse , double taxation due to the payment for waste management on importation and at post-consumer waste level , and a 10-fold increase of the registration fees . " <p> Abak has asked the parliamentary committee to annul the regulations and ask the Ministry of Environment to conduct an impact assessment of the new charges and involve all stakeholders in the development of regulations that would promote manufacturing . <p> " A great deal of investment in infrastructure and consumer sensitisation is required to achieve 100 per cent sustainable waste management , " Githua said . <p> " This can only be achieved over time and through strong collaboration between government and all stakeholders within the manufacturing ecosystem. @@118865965 <p> The surge in commercial interest is a double-edged sword . The high demand for baobab pulp raises serious concerns about interference with natural regeneration , loss of genetic diversity and the health of baobab populations/FILE <p> Baobab trees grow in arid and semi-arid parts of Africa , and have deep cultural and ecological significance . Some of these trees are thousands of years old . Global demand for baobab products has surged in recent years , driven by the fruit 's nutritional and health benefits . <p> However , this growing demand has become a double-edged sword , raising concerns about the sustainability of these ancient trees . Patrick Maundu , who is part of a research project to document the heritage of the baobab tree in Kenya , explains what can be done to protect the tree for future generations . <h> Where are baobabs found ? <p> The tree is native to at least 37 countries in Africa and two in the Arabian Peninsula . The African baobab ( Adansonia digitata ) is the most widespread of the eight known baobab species . It endures in some of the harshest conditions , from salty water-bathed ocean shores to vast dry savannahs and forests . Of the rest of the baobabs , six are native to Madagascar , and one to northern and western Australia . <p> Baobabs are unique trees . They are among the world 's longest-living trees , with some being over 2,000 years old . They can survive prolonged droughts thanks to their ability to store water in their huge trunks , which can attain a diameter of 10 metres or more . The trunk has amazing regenerative ability , easily growing back after damage by humans in search of fibre or from wildlife like elephants trying to quench their thirst . <p> Unlike most trees , which are adorned with lush leaves , the baobab often stands bare for the greater part of the year . Its thick , leafless branches stretch out like skeletal arms , creating a weird and almost mystical appearance . <p> The tree has a slow growth rate . It starts to flower and produce fruits from about 20 years of age . In many cases , however , especially in arid regions , trees give their first fruit much later , with some known to start at 60 years . <h> What is the baobab 's significance ? <p> In African cultures , every part of the baobab is valuable . <p> The leaves are consumed as a nutritious vegetable . The fruit pulp , with its tangy taste , adds flavour to foods and beverages . Baobab pulp is rich in antioxidants , vitamins ( like C and B complex ) , fibre and minerals ( such as calcium , iron and magnesium ) . <p> Advertisement . Scroll to continue reading . <p> The seeds are a source of oil in the cosmetic industry . The inner bark is harvested for its fibre , which is woven into ropes and baskets . Hollows in the trunk provide shelter for honey bees . The fruit shell is made into utensils and other household items . The baobab 's bark and root extracts are widely used in traditional medicine . <p> Because of its value , the baobab has attracted folklore and myths . Many African communities consider the tree sacred , often associating it with spirits . As a result , various ceremonies and rituals are conducted under it . <p> Ecologically , the baobab holds an important position in the landscape , supporting a wide range of wildlife , including fungi , insects , birds , reptiles , bats and monkeys . Its large trunk serves as a water reservoir . The elaborate root system stabilises the soil , preventing erosion . Fallen leaves enrich the soil with nutrients . <p> Additionally , the baobab acts as a carbon sink . This means it absorbs carbon dioxide from the atmosphere and packs it away , helping mitigate climate change . <h> Why is global demand for baobabs spiking ? <p> The baobab 's reputation as a superfood is spreading . This has mainly been fuelled by the recognition of baobab pulp as a food ingredient by the European Union and the US Food and Drug Administration in 2008 and 2009 , respectively . This opened the way for its use as an ingredient in drinks , foods , natural remedies and cosmetics . <p> Zimbabwe has become a trailblazer in this industry , aggressively targeting the lucrative European market . <h> Why is the tree under threat ? <p> The baobab is under threat on several fronts . Its slow growth rate , huge size , long life and economic value expose the tree to many risks . Although some Madagascan baobab species are listed as either critically endangered or endangered , the African baobab is not . Yet , there is evidence that specific unique populations may be declining in parts of Africa , calling for more conscious conservation measures to be taken . <p> Climate change is already reshaping the ecosystems baobabs rely on . These ancient giants need a specific range of soil and air humidity . They also depend on specific pollinators , like bats and bush babies , for reproduction . However , rising temperatures and shifting rainfall patterns are disrupting these balances , affecting the overall health of the tree and reproductive capacity . <p> Changing community belief systems and local values are having an effect , too . The baobab 's sacred status is waning as modernity spreads . In some communities , the tree is now seen as a relic of the past . Further , the baobab 's expansive canopy and roots compete for space and nutrients with food crops amid shrinking agricultural space . This has intensified the tendency of communities to cut the tree . <p> Advertisement . Scroll to continue reading . <p> The surge in commercial interest is a double-edged sword . The high demand for baobab pulp raises serious concerns about interference with natural regeneration , loss of genetic diversity and the health of baobab populations . Baobab is still harvested using crude methods -- such as hitting the fruits from the ground or climbing on pegs inserted into the stem -- that harm the tree . <p> This commercial interest has brought a new threat : biopiracy . This was witnessed in Kenya in 2022 when entire baobab trees were controversially uprooted and exported to Georgia in eastern Europe . Eight trees were exported but later died , signalling the lack of prior research about their viability in their new home . <p> This incident highlighted the lack of appropriate policies and regulatory frameworks to protect these important trees from exploitation , and underscored the urgent need for specific policies on safeguarding the baobab . <h> What should be done ? <p> Safeguarding the baobab requires more than isolated efforts . <p> It demands a blend of cultural and community protection , and conservation and management actions at the community level . It also requires strategic policy and regulatory frameworks , and collaboration on the national and global stages . <p> These policies should also support livelihood programmes for communities by supporting value chains and providing market linkages for baobab products . Promoting sustainable harvesting techniques , like leaving enough fruit for regeneration , will protect the trees and surrounding environments . Integrating indigenous knowledge with tools like genetic research will enhance these efforts . <p> James Kioko , who is part of the research team working with Dr Maundu on documenting the heritage of the baobab in Kenya , is a co-author of this article . <h> Kenya <h> More on Capital News <p> This website uses cookies to improve your experience . We 'll assume you 're ok with this , but you can opt-out if you wish . @@118884510 <p> The Standard Group Plc is a multi-media organization with investments in media platforms spanning newspaper print operations , television , radio broadcasting , digital and online services . The Standard Group is recognized as a leading multi-media house in Kenya with a key influence in matters of national and international interest . <h> Public officials ' communication should be clear <h> JavaScript is disabled ! <p> The planned vaccination of cows is an example of how not to communicate a government policy . President William Ruto , being a scientist , could have easily explained himself to the people . <p> Instead he chose to lambast everyone opposed to the exercise . The President never needed to speak to the matter as a passing statement but could have issued an elaborate statement that acknowledges and assuages people 's fears . <p> Listening to Agriculture CS Andrew Karanja , a few days ago , one gets a feeling that what the government is pursuing and what people are concerned about are not the same thing , yet you can not blame the people for this . <p> Kenyans ' concern emanates from a controversial plan that aims to reduce cow emissions . Research shows that cows contribute a great deal to climate change by producing methane , a gas said to be 28 times more potent in global warming . <p> However , according to CS Karanja , the planned vaccination has nothing to do with this controversial plan . Instead , he said the vaccination is meant to address common diseases like foot and mouth disease . He also reiterated that the vaccines are not new and have been manufactured in Kenya over the past 12 years . This is the clarity Kenyans are always seeking . They need not be called stupid and mad . <p> The fears Kenyans are expressing are real . No serious farmer does n't have a vaccination and treatment schedule for his or her cows even without the government 's input . When there is an outbreak of a particular disease , it would be in the news , and any farmer would have a veterinarian on speed dial . So the government must really come clear on what exactly it is intending to do . <p> At the moment , the only intervention approved in reducing the amount of methane produced by cows is through a food additive known as Bovaer . These additives are meant to interrupt the feed fermentation process in a cow 's rumen , thus reducing the amount of methane gas produced by a significant percentage . <p> Another product funded by renowned tycoon Bill Gates , Amazon founder Jeff Bezos and Alibaba founder Jack Ma known as Rumin8 is at the testing stage , and Kenyans are worried whether their cows will be the testing ground . <p> What the government forgets when communicating is that this information is available online , and Kenyans sometimes land on it even before the mainstream media does . Such information would be confusing , particularly if the government coincidentally decides to run a mass vaccination programme . What would make anyone believe that the government is communicating genuinely when all they get is insults ? <p> One principle of communication is that ' communication is what is heard and not what is said . ' The people will not just hear the message but will also judge credibility of the person communicating , the tone and other attendant factors . If the tone is off , the communication will also be off .

@@118934112 <h> Suggestions <h> How to Write a Synthesis Essay : A Step-by-Step Guide for Students <h> Definition of a Synthesis Essay <p> A synthesis essay is an analytical piece that combines various sources of information to present a coherent discussion on a particular topic . <p> This type of essay goes beyond summarizing existing materials . Instead , it demonstrates critical thinking by connecting ideas from multiple texts to form a well-rounded argument or response . <p> Writing a synthesis essay , much like when you order admission essay services from OrderEssay.com , requires careful analysis and the ability to weave different perspectives into a unified narrative . <h> Purpose of Writing a Synthesis Essay <p> The purpose of writing a synthesis essay is multifaceted : <p> To engage with diverse perspectives <p> To deepen understanding of a subject <p> To develop analytical skills <p> For instance , while researching climate change , a synthesis essay allows one to evaluate scientific findings , activist viewpoints , and governmental policies , creating a comprehensive discourse surrounding the issue . This highlights the importance of synthesis writing in developing well-rounded arguments . <h> Understanding the Prompt <h> Analyzing the Topic <p> Understanding the prompt is crucial for crafting a compelling synthesis essay . Start by carefully analyzing the topic to ensure a focused argument . Consider questions such as : <p> What are the main themes ? <p> What assumptions are embedded in the topic ? <p> By dissecting these elements , you clarify your direction and purpose in writing a synthesis essay . <h> Identifying the Key Points <p> Once you have a grasp on the topic , the next step is identifying the key points you need to address . This involves pinpointing essential arguments , evidence , and counter-arguments that will enrich your essay . <p> Create a list of main ideas : This helps in organizing thoughts effectively . <p> By developing a clear note-taking system , such as using digital tools or color-coded index cards , you can streamline the synthesis process and stay organized . <h> Developing a Thesis Statement <h> Formulating the Main Argument <p> After gathering information , the next crucial step is developing a strong thesis statement . This serves as the backbone of your essay , encapsulating your main argument . Aim for clarity and specificity . <p> Be direct : State your position clearly . <p> Focus on a single idea : Avoid convoluted statements . <p> For instance , if arguing the benefits of remote work , you might say , " Remote work increases employee productivity and job satisfaction . " <h> Incorporating Different Viewpoints <p> A well-rounded thesis not only states your position but also acknowledges opposing views . This approach enriches your argument , demonstrating critical thinking . Consider : <p> For example , you might write , " Technology enhances collaborative learning ; however , it can also distract students . " These transitions not only improve flow but also enhance comprehension for your audience . <h> Editing and Revising <h> Checking for Clarity and Coherence <p> After drafting your synthesis essay , the next step is editing and revising . Start by checking for clarity and coherence . Ensure each idea flows logically to the next . Consider : <p> Revising awkward sentences : Simplify complex phrases . <p> Confirming paragraph structure : Each paragraph should revolve around a single point . <p> For example , if a paragraph feels jumbled , break it down into two or more clearer segments . <h> Proofreading for Errors <p> Once you feel satisfied with the clarity and structure , it 's time to proofread for errors . This final step is crucial to ensure your work is polished . Focus on : 
@@118943049 <h> Funny <p> Ecuadorian bananas remain one of the country 's export mainstays , with a market characterized by complex challenges and key opportunities for the future . The Cavendish variety , grown all year round , accounts for the bulk of exports to destinations such as the United States , Europe , the Middle East , and the Southern Cone . <p> The last two years have been particularly difficult for Ecuadorian grower-exporters , who are dealing with high input costs and spot prices that exceed the values agreed in international contracts . According to Lorena Quisilema , Operations Manager at Andradecomex , " Ecuador does not have its own currency , which makes it unattractive compared to other banana producing countries , and the high operational , production and logistical costs result in difficulties in closing negotiations with potential clients . " <p> Despite the difficulties , the sector shows signs of resilience and ambition . Andradecomex , which started exporting in 2024 , is currently shipping between 10,000 and 20,000 boxes of bananas per week , with plans to increase this volume to 30,000 to 40,000 boxes in the coming years . With this growth , the company seeks to position Ecuadorian bananas in the global market , highlighting their traceability and high-quality standards that have earned them international certifications . <p> Climate change has also taken a toll on the productivity of the farms and reduced the projected harvests ; however , constant investment and the proper use of good agricultural practices seek to mitigate this while also ensuring sustainability and boosting production . <p> Striving to diversify , the company is also betting on exotic products such as pineapple and pitaya . Although the market for the latter has been affected by overproduction and low prices , there is optimism in its growth potential . <p> Bananas , however , remain in the spotlight . " Our goal is to position our brand worldwide and be known for our quality and good agricultural practices , " says Quisilema . This approach seeks not only to ensure the sector 's growth but also to have a positive impact on the economy of the country 's agricultural communities .

It is regrettable that farmers have not been able to optimize production due to poor breeds , but this is now set to be a thing of the past , " said the Chief Officer . <p> The county produces 25,000 to 30,000 litres of milk in a day . <p> Dr Cheruiyot added that through the initiative , the Department 's sub-county officers have been provided with semen , nitrogen tanks , and technical expertise to offer AI services to dairy farmers . <p> He said that the program is a game changer since it will ensure a thriving dairy and beef industry . <p> Dr Cheruiyot added the county will continue to have continuous capacity building training for the cooperatives to ensure success of the program . <p> He advised farmers to join the Nakuru County Cooperative Dairy Union as it brings together 15 giant and active cooperatives . The major aim of the Union , the Chief Officer noted , is to promote value addition and marketing for better prices . <p> " The ultimate goal of this programme is to ensure that farmers increase their milk and meat production and get more value from it so that they can uplift their living standards , " he said . <p> The initiative , he said , will significantly reduce the cost of AI services , while at the same time improving the quality of the breeds the farmers get ; which in turn will translate into increased milk production when the calves mature to start milk production . <p> Dr Cheruiyot asked farmers to adopt zero grazing as a way of controlling pests and diseases . <p> He reiterated that agriculture remains the backbone of the economy and a crucial driver of livelihoods , not only in Nakuru County but across the nation . <p> " With these digital advancements , the future of agriculture in Nakuru County looks promising . The awards celebrate and support African women scientists for the quality of their scientific research . <p> This year , 25 PhD candidates and 5 post-doctoral researchers from 15 African countries were rewarded for their innovative work and commitment to addressing major scientific challenges threatening the continent such as food security , public health , and the impact of climate change . <p> These trailblazers in science were selected among nearly 800 applications by a jury chaired by Professor Aggrey AMBALI , Head of Technical Cooperation and Program Financing at the African Union Development Agency ( AUDA-NEPAD ) . <p> The award ceremony took place in Cotonou , Benin , on December 10th , 2024 , with the presence of government representatives of Benin . Yet , we are merely at their threshold . To address those challenges , we need science more than ever ; however , women who wish to pursue scientific careers are battling headwinds of obstacles while they can bring adapted perspectives , boundless innovative capabilities , and unwavering commitment to everyone 's development . Herein lies the reason why we have been supporting women scientists in Sub-Saharan Africa for 15 years , alongside with UNESCO , to help them develop their leadership and design a more inclusive future . Much ground remains to be covered , and our efforts must remain steadfast , " stated Alexandra Palt , Vice President of the Fondation L'Or ? al . <p> According to Lidia Brito , UNESCO Assistant Director-General for Natural Sciences , " one of the greatest strengths and potential of the African continent is youth . Support for UNICEF 's emergency response can be made through flexible funding and Mozambique 's humanitarian appeal . UNICEF Mozambique urgently needs US$10 million to respond to the multiple emergencies caused by Cyclone Chido 's devastation .

According to the Integrated Food Security Phase Classification ( IPC ) report released in November , 6.4 million people are severely food-insecure in crisis levels or worse ( IPC Phase 3+ ) . @@119008035 <h> Climate action : Key highlights of COP meetings <p> During this year 's meeting , participants reached a breakthrough agreement that will triple finance to developing countries . <p> The new finance goal at COP29 builds on significant strides forward on global climate action at COP27 . <p> COP 29 , Africa 's opportunity in global carbon markets <p> Every year , leaders from across the world meet to discuss ways how to tackle the effects posed by climate change which is now a threat to millions of lives around the globe . <p> The key to achieving this is to come up with solutions to reduce greenhouse gas emissions which must get to zero . <p> Discussions among these leaders have been around changing how we get our energy to limiting deforestation . <p> The United Nations Climate Change Conference , known as COP ( Conference of the Parties ) , is the summit of leaders , policymakers , scientists , and activists to discuss these issues . <p> These conferences are critical for setting international goals , creating frameworks , and committing to actions that tackle global warming , reduce carbon emissions , and address the ecological damage caused by climate change . <p> COP meetings are held under the framework of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change ( UNFCCC ) , with each session being numbered as COP27 and COP28 . <p> That means the global economy must " mitigate " climate change -- in other words , we must reduce or prevent the emission of greenhouse gases to get us to where science says we need to be by 2030 . <p> In line with that , a mitigation work programme was established in Sharm el-Sheikh , aimed at urgently scaling up mitigation ambition and implementation . <p> The work programme will start immediately and continue until 2026 when there will be a review to consider its extension . <p> Holding businesses and institutions to account <p> UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres said net-zero commitments can not be a mere public relations exercise if we want to win the fight against climate change . <p> " We must have zero tolerance for greenwashing , " he said . <p> This new phase of implementation also means a new focus on accountability when it comes to the commitments made by sectors , businesses , and institutions . <p> Mobilizing more financial support for developing countries <p> Finance is at the heart of all that the world is doing to combat climate change . <p> Mitigation , adaptation , loss and damage , climate technology -- all of it requires sufficient funds to function properly and to yield the desired results . <p> On this crucial topic , COP27 created a pathway to align the broader finance flows towards low emissions and climate-resilient development . <p> The COP27 cover decision , known as the Sharm el-Sheikh Implementation Plan , highlights that a global transformation to a low-carbon economy is expected to require investments of at least USD 4-6 trillion a year . <p> Delivering such funding will require a swift and comprehensive transformation of the financial system and its structures and processes , engaging governments , central banks , commercial banks , institutional investors and other financial actors . <p> One of the key outcomes of the various decisions on climate finance is a call for developed country Parties to provide resources for the second replenishment of the Green Climate Fund . <p> Notably , nations resolved to make the transition to low-emission and climate-resilient development ambitious , just and equitable . <p> They went one step further at COP27 by deciding to establish a work programme on ' just transition , ' which is expected to build on and complement the work to urgently scale up mitigation ambition and implementation . <p> COP28 ( 2023 ) <p> It was held in Dubai , United Arab Emirates ( UAE ) It took place from November 30 to December 12 , 2023 . <p> COP28 was the biggest of its kind bringing together some 85,000 participants , including more than 150 Heads of State and Government . <p> Having shown that progress was too slow across all areas of climate action , countries responded with a decision on how to accelerate action across all areas by 2030 . <p> Key takeaways <p> New funding for Loss and Damage <p> The conference got underway with a historic agreement on the operationalization of funding arrangements for addressing loss and damage . <p> This includes a new dedicated fund under the UNFCCC -- the first time a substantive decision was adopted on the first day of the conference . <p> Commitments to address loss and damage started coming in moments after the decision was made , totalling more than USD 600 million to date . <p> Enhancing global efforts to strengthen resilience <p> Parties agreed on targets for the Global Goal on Adaptation ( GGA ) and its framework , which identify where the world needs to get to to be resilient to the impacts of a changing climate and assess countries ' efforts . <p> The decision gives adaptation progress a future orientation for the first time , reflecting aspiration and ambition , as opposed to the previous practice of measurement against past efforts . <p> Linking Climate Action with Conservation <p> Governments were called on to consider ecosystems , biodiversity and carbon stores , such as forests , when developing their stronger national climate action plans ( known as naturally determined contributions ) , which are due by early 2025 . <p> This call was part of a wide-ranging , comprehensive decision by parties on the world 's first ' global stocktake ' to ratchet up climate action before the end of the decade to limit the global temperature rise to 1.5 ? C. <p> COP 29 ( 2024 ) <p> It was held in Baku , Azerbaijan . <p> It took place between November 14-22 and brought together nearly 200 countries from around the world . <p> The meeting closed with a new finance goal to help countries to protect their people and economies against climate disasters , and share in the vast benefits of the clean energy boom . <p> Key takeaways <p> With a central focus on climate finance , the participants also reached a breakthrough agreement that will triple finance to developing countries , from the previous goal of USD 100 billion annually , to USD 300 billion annually by 2035 . <p> It will also secure efforts of all actors to work together to scale up finance to developing countries , from public and private sources to the amount of USD 1.3 trillion per year by 2035 . <p> Known formally as the New Collective Quantified Goal on Climate Finance ( NCQG ) , it was agreed upon after two weeks of intensive negotiations and several years of preparatory work , in a process that requires all nations to unanimously agree on every word of the agreement . <p> The new finance goal at COP29 builds on significant strides forward on global climate action at COP27 , which agreed to a historic Loss and Damage Fund , and COP28 , which delivered a global agreement to transition away from all fossil fuels in energy systems swiftly and fairly , triple renewable energy and boost climate resilience . <p> COP29 also reached an agreement on carbon markets -- which several previous COPs had not been able to achieve . <p> These agreements will help countries deliver their climate plans more quickly and cheaply , and make faster progress in halving global emissions this decade , as required by science .

@@119017628 <h> Funny <p> Peru expected to produce more than 323,000 tons of blueberries in the 2024/25 season <h> " The growth of the Peruvian blueberry is no coincidence " <p> Peru is expected to produce 323,928 tons of blueberries in the 2024-2025 season , exceeding initial estimates by 4.1% , and consolidating the country as the world leading blueberry exporter . Luis Miguel Vegas , general manager of Proar ? ndanos , affirms that this success is the result of a comprehensive strategy : " The growth of the Peruvian blueberry is no coincidence ; it is the result of a joint effort to diversify markets , invest in quality , and respond to consumer demands . " <p> Historically , the United States has been the main destination for Peruvian blueberries , receiving 53% of exports ( 135,000 tons ) . However , diversification is a priority . " Today we export Peruvian blueberries to more than 20 countries , with Europe and China as key growth markets , " Vegas said . " This change has reduced dependence on a single market , with Europe accounting for 25% , China 14% , and the United Kingdom 5% of the total volume exported . " <p> " Logistics plays a crucial role in this global expansion , with 97% of shipments being made by sea . The main destination ports , such as Philadelphia , Rotterdam , and Shenzhen , are strategic to maintain the freshness and quality of the product . " " Peru 's logistical success lies in our ability to efficiently connect producing regions with global markets , " Vegas stressed . <p> One of the drivers of growth has been the commitment to more competitive varieties . In 2016 , Biloxi dominated the market with 58% of the certified hectares . Today , this figure has fallen to 16% , while Ventura leads with 26% , followed by Sekoya Pop with 14% . " The evolution of the varieties reflects our ability to adapt . We innovate to respond to market demands and increase our competitiveness , " Vegas stated . <p> In addition , organic blueberries have gained ground , accounting for 11% of current exports and with a projected volume of 40,000 tons at the end of the season . This growth reaffirms the sector 's commitment to sustainability and modern consumer preferences . <p> Despite progress , the sector faces significant challenges , such as pressure for competitive prices and the impact of climate change . " The biggest challenge is to maintain our competitiveness without compromising on quality . This is only possible through innovation and a sustainable approach , " Vegas stressed . <p> Blueberries have transformed production regions such as La Libertad , Lambayeque , and Ica , which together account for 85% of national production . Thousands of direct and indirect jobs depend on this industry , consolidating it as an economic pillar for the country . " Each ton exported not only represents income for Peru but also opportunities for thousands of families , " Vegas said .

The emphasis on reducing government spending and potential cuts to farm safety net programs could further impact small and historically marginalized farmers . <p> As the agricultural community navigates these challenges , the necessity for a robust support system and sustainable practices becomes increasingly apparent , aiming to safeguard the future of farming amidst a changing climate and economic landscape .
